People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess… what finally pushed me to come in is that I don’t feel like myself in this very quiet way. Like the part of me that used to reach for people, or get something back from my work, has sort of gone dim.

I’m still doing what I’m supposed to do, mostly. I go through obligations. But yoga, galleries, journaling—things that used to feel like they filled me up—now I just don’t really… feel much from them. It’s strange, because I used to tell clients all the time about small routines and connection and meaning, and now I’ll buy a new notebook and it just sits there. Actually, I have this whole drawer of notebooks, which is ridiculous, but anyway… that’s the main thing. I feel like I’m moving through things rather than living in them.

I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. Empty maybe… but even that word feels a little too neat. It’s more like I’m muted.

I’ll have moments where I’m fine, like if I’m making tea or noticing some weird little thing outside the window, and then I think, “See, I’m okay.” But then the rest of the day feels kind of flat.

Sorry, I’m not answering cleanly. I guess I haven’t been crying all the time or anything. It’s more just… not much seems to land.

But yoga, galleries, journaling—things that used to feel like they filled me up—now I just don’t really… feel much from them. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Worthless… I don’t know. That’s a heavy word. I wouldn’t say I walk around thinking that exactly.

Guilty, maybe in the normal way? Like if I forget to call someone back, or I snap because the kitchen is too crowded, then I’ll replay it longer than I probably need to. But I don’t know if that’s “excessive” or just… being a person in a small apartment with everyone’s habits rubbing up against each other.

Hopeless about the future… I don’t really think that far ahead lately. Not in a dramatic way. It’s more like the future is sort of foggy, like when you’re trying to plan dinner but you first have to move three things off the counter, and then you notice the mail pile, and suddenly it’s not about dinner anymore. So… I’m not sure I’d call it hopeless. I haven’t really noticed thoughts like that clearly.

I’m still showing up, mostly. Like, I haven’t disappeared or stopped doing the basic things. I get dressed, I go to work, I answer emails… eventually.

But it’s not exactly “as usual.” There have been a couple grant deadlines I missed, which is embarrassing because that used to be the kind of thing I could keep in my head, or at least on three different sticky notes. And my supervisor hasn’t said it outright in a harsh way, but she’s… shifted some of my more complicated clients to other clinicians. Quietly. Like she’s trying not to make it a thing.

So I guess I’m functioning, but not at my normal level. It’s hard to say that without wanting to explain it away.

So… I’m not sure I’d call it hopeless. I haven’t really noticed thoughts like that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Over these past eight months, has this 'muted' feeling actually interfered with your ability to do your job, care for your family, or manage daily responsibilities, or have you still been able to keep up with everything as usual?\n\nPatient: I’m still showing up, mostly. Like, I haven’t disappeared or stopped doing the basic things. I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clean difference like that. I mean, work has its own noise, you know? The printer, people knocking, the little crisis pile that builds up before lunch… so I can’t say I feel suddenly lighter just because I’m not at the apartment.

So… I don’t know. Maybe I’m a little less caught in it when I’m fully occupied somewhere else, but I haven’t tracked it enough to say. At home I’m more aware of everything that needs doing, but that might just be because I’m there looking at it.

clinicians because I’m not functioning at my usual level.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 8 months\n  manifestation: This whole change in how I’m thinking, working, and connecting with people has been going on for about eight months, since we took my older relative into our apartment.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 2 months\n  manifestation: The more clearly unsettling parts, like feeling that emails and hallway pauses are directed at me, have been present for about the past two months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / mental_health_or_substance_abuse_social_worker / graduate\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S023\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that you're worthless, or feeling excessively guilty about things, or feeling hopeless about the future during these past eight months?\n\nPatient: Worthless… I don’t know. That’s a heavy word. I wouldn’t say I walk around thinking that exactly.\n\nGuilty, maybe in the normal way? Like if I forget to call someone back, or I snap because the kitchen is too crowded, then I’ll replay it longer than I probably need to. But I don’t know if that’s “excessive” or just… being a person in a small apartment with everyone’s habits rubbing up against each other.\n\nHopeless about the future… I don’t really think that far ahead lately. Not in a dramatic way. It’s more like the future is sort of foggy, like when you’re trying to plan dinner but you first have to move three things off the counter, and then you notice the mail pile, and suddenly it’s not about dinner anymore. So… I’m not sure I’d call it hopeless. I haven’t really noticed thoughts like that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Over these past eight months, has this 'muted' feeling actually interfered with your ability to do your job, care for your family, or manage daily responsibilities, or have you still been able to keep up with everything as usual?\n\nPatient: I’m still showing up, mostly. Like, I haven’t disappeared or stopped doing the basic things. I get dressed, I go to work, I answer emails… eventually.\n\nBut it’s not exactly “as usual.” There have been a couple grant deadlines I missed, which is embarrassing because that used to be the kind of thing I could keep in my head, or at least on three different sticky notes. And my supervisor hasn’t said it outright in a harsh way, but she’s… shifted some of my more complicated clients to other clinicians. Quietly. Like she’s trying not to make it a thing.\n\nSo I guess I’m functioning, but not at my normal level. It’s hard to say that without wanting to explain it away.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difference in how you feel on days or times when you're away from the apartment and the caregiving situation—for example, during a weekend trip or a day fully at work—versus when you're at home dealing with it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clean difference like that. I mean, work has its own noise, you know? The printer, people knocking, the little crisis pile that builds up before lunch… so I can’t say I feel suddenly lighter just because I’m not at the apartment.\n\nAnd I haven’t really had, like, a real weekend trip where I could compare it. I keep thinking we should go somewhere, even just a cheap motel with bad coffee, but then there’s the question of who covers what, and whether the cat feeder still works—sorry.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe I’m a little less caught in it when I’m fully occupied somewhere else, but I haven’t tracked it enough to say. At home I’m more aware of everything that needs doing, but that might just be because I’m there looking at it.\n\n[Last Question]\nFocusing just on sleep: compared to eight months ago, are you now taking longer to fall asleep, waking up in the middle of the night, or waking up much earlier than you want to, on most nights?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Sleep… I’m not sure I have a clean answer for that. I mean, I go to bed, and sometimes I’m thinking about whether I set the coffee up or if the laundry is still in the washer, and then I’ll remember something totally unrelated, like this one client years ago who always wore purple scarves—sorry, that’s not what you asked.

Compared to eight months ago… I haven’t really been tracking it. I don’t think I’m waking up dramatically early every day or anything that obvious. Falling asleep maybe depends on the night? Some nights I’m out, some nights I lie there a while, but I couldn’t honestly say it’s most nights or that it’s a big change.

So… I guess I haven’t noticed a clear sleep pattern change.

people and take pleasure in my work, yoga, galleries, even journaling, has gone very quiet, and I’m mostly just moving through obligations.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’m still showing up to work, but I’ve missed grant deadlines and my supervisor has quietly moved some of my more demanding clients to other clinicians because I’m not functioning at my usual level.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 8 months\n  manifestation: This whole change in how I’m thinking, working, and connecting with people has been going on for about eight months, since we took my older relative into our apartment.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 2 months\n  manifestation: The more clearly unsettling parts, like feeling that emails and hallway pauses are directed at me, have been present for about the past two months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / mental_health_or_substance_abuse_social_worker / graduate\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S023\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Over these past eight months, has this 'muted' feeling actually interfered with your ability to do your job, care for your family, or manage daily responsibilities, or have you still been able to keep up with everything as usual?\n\nPatient: I’m still showing up, mostly. Like, I haven’t disappeared or stopped doing the basic things. I get dressed, I go to work, I answer emails… eventually.\n\nBut it’s not exactly “as usual.” There have been a couple grant deadlines I missed, which is embarrassing because that used to be the kind of thing I could keep in my head, or at least on three different sticky notes. And my supervisor hasn’t said it outright in a harsh way, but she’s… shifted some of my more complicated clients to other clinicians. Quietly. Like she’s trying not to make it a thing.\n\nSo I guess I’m functioning, but not at my normal level. It’s hard to say that without wanting to explain it away.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difference in how you feel on days or times when you're away from the apartment and the caregiving situation—for example, during a weekend trip or a day fully at work—versus when you're at home dealing with it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clean difference like that. I mean, work has its own noise, you know? The printer, people knocking, the little crisis pile that builds up before lunch… so I can’t say I feel suddenly lighter just because I’m not at the apartment.\n\nAnd I haven’t really had, like, a real weekend trip where I could compare it. I keep thinking we should go somewhere, even just a cheap motel with bad coffee, but then there’s the question of who covers what, and whether the cat feeder still works—sorry.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe I’m a little less caught in it when I’m fully occupied somewhere else, but I haven’t tracked it enough to say. At home I’m more aware of everything that needs doing, but that might just be because I’m there looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Focusing just on sleep: compared to eight months ago, are you now taking longer to fall asleep, waking up in the middle of the night, or waking up much earlier than you want to, on most nights?\n\nPatient: Sleep… I’m not sure I have a clean answer for that. I mean, I go to bed, and sometimes I’m thinking about whether I set the coffee up or if the laundry is still in the washer, and then I’ll remember something totally unrelated, like this one client years ago who always wore purple scarves—sorry, that’s not what you asked.\n\nCompared to eight months ago… I haven’t really been tracking it. I don’t think I’m waking up dramatically early every day or anything that obvious. Falling asleep maybe depends on the night? Some nights I’m out, some nights I lie there a while, but I couldn’t honestly say it’s most nights or that it’s a big change.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed a clear sleep pattern change.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any change in your appetite or your weight—either eating more or less than usual, or gaining or losing weight—over these past eight months?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Actually, I have this whole drawer of notebooks, which is ridiculous, but anyway… that’s the main thing. I keep trying to pinpoint a day, but it wasn’t really like that—it was more like the apartment changed, the routines changed, and then I looked up and realized I’d changed too.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the loss of interest and pleasure, have you also been feeling sad, empty, or down for most of the day, nearly every day, during these past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. months\n  manifestation: The more clearly unsettling parts, like feeling that emails and hallway pauses are directed at me, have been present for about the past two months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / mental_health_or_substance_abuse_social_worker / graduate\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S023\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difference in how you feel on days or times when you're away from the apartment and the caregiving situation—for example, during a weekend trip or a day fully at work—versus when you're at home dealing with it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clean difference like that. I mean, work has its own noise, you know? The printer, people knocking, the little crisis pile that builds up before lunch… so I can’t say I feel suddenly lighter just because I’m not at the apartment.\n\nAnd I haven’t really had, like, a real weekend trip where I could compare it. I keep thinking we should go somewhere, even just a cheap motel with bad coffee, but then there’s the question of who covers what, and whether the cat feeder still works—sorry.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe I’m a little less caught in it when I’m fully occupied somewhere else, but I haven’t tracked it enough to say. At home I’m more aware of everything that needs doing, but that might just be because I’m there looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Focusing just on sleep: compared to eight months ago, are you now taking longer to fall asleep, waking up in the middle of the night, or waking up much earlier than you want to, on most nights?\n\nPatient: Sleep… I’m not sure I have a clean answer for that. I mean, I go to bed, and sometimes I’m thinking about whether I set the coffee up or if the laundry is still in the washer, and then I’ll remember something totally unrelated, like this one client years ago who always wore purple scarves—sorry, that’s not what you asked.\n\nCompared to eight months ago… I haven’t really been tracking it. I don’t think I’m waking up dramatically early every day or anything that obvious. Falling asleep maybe depends on the night? Some nights I’m out, some nights I lie there a while, but I couldn’t honestly say it’s most nights or that it’s a big change.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed a clear sleep pattern change.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your appetite or your weight—either eating more or less than usual, or gaining or losing weight—over these past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? Not in a way I’ve really noticed. My appetite is… kind of ordinary, I guess. Some days I forget lunch because the day gets away from me, but that happened before too, especially if I’m doing paperwork and then suddenly it’s 3:40 and the yogurt I brought is still sitting there.\n\nWeight-wise, I don’t weigh myself regularly. We have a scale but it’s under a stack of towels in the bathroom because the closet shelf broke—well, not broke, exactly, it’s just sagging. Anyway, my clothes fit about the same, so I don’t think there’s been a big gain or loss. Nothing obvious.\n\n[Last Question]\nRegardless of motivation, have you been feeling physically tired or low in energy nearly every day over these past eight months—for example, feeling drained even after resting?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I’d call it nearly every day. I mean, I get tired, sure, but everyone says that, don’t they? Especially by the end of the day when there’s still dishes and the mail pile and that one envelope you keep moving from table to table instead of opening.

But drained even after resting… I’m not sure. Sometimes I rest and then I just feel sort of annoyed that I used up the time resting, which is different from being physically exhausted, I think. I haven’t noticed a clear, constant low-energy thing over the whole eight months. Nothing I could point to and say, “Yes, that’s happening almost every day.”

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F32.8, F43.20\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, welcome in—thanks for coming today. I'm Dr. [Name]. To start, can you tell me what's been bringing you in to see me?\n\nPatient: I guess… what finally pushed me to come in is that I don’t feel like myself in this very quiet way. Like the part of me that used to reach for people, or get something back from my work, has sort of gone dim.\n\nI’m still doing what I’m supposed to do, mostly. I go through obligations. But yoga, galleries, journaling—things that used to feel like they filled me up—now I just don’t really… feel much from them. It’s strange, because I used to tell clients all the time about small routines and connection and meaning, and now I’ll buy a new notebook and it just sits there. Actually, I have this whole drawer of notebooks, which is ridiculous, but anyway… that’s the main thing. I feel like I’m moving through things rather than living in them.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—weeks, months, or longer?\n\nPatient: I’d say… months. About eight months, I think. It started around when we took my older relative into our apartment, and since then the whole way I’m thinking and working and being with people just feels different. I keep trying to pinpoint a day, but it wasn’t really like that—it was more like the apartment changed, the routines changed, and then I looked up and realized I’d changed too.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the loss of interest and pleasure, have you also been feeling sad, empty, or down for most of the day, nearly every day, during these past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. Empty maybe… but even that word feels a little too neat. It’s more like I’m muted.\n\nMost of the day, nearly every day—when you say it like that, I start trying to calculate it, which is probably not helpful. I’ll have moments where I’m fine, like if I’m making tea or noticing some weird little thing outside the window, and then I think, “See, I’m okay.” But then the rest of the day feels kind of flat.\n\nSorry, I’m not answering cleanly. I guess I haven’t been crying all the time or anything. It’s more just… not much seems to land.\n\nDoctor: Besides feeling muted or flat, have you noticed changes in your energy, concentration, sleep, or appetite over these past eight months?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure I can separate those out very well. Like, energy versus motivation versus just being tired of the same rooms, you know?\n\nSleep—well, I notice it more when my neighbor’s hallway light flickers under the door, which is such a small thing, but once I see it I can’t unsee it. And then I start thinking about whether we should change the curtains, or whether the plant near the window is dying because I moved it. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t been tracking appetite or anything. Concentration… sometimes I feel like I’m reading the same paragraph twice, but I don’t know if that’s new or just me being distracted. So, I don’t have a clear answer. Nothing where I thought, “Oh, this is a big change,” exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that you're worthless, or feeling excessively guilty about things, or feeling hopeless about the future during these past eight months?\n\nPatient: Worthless… I don’t know. That’s a heavy word. I wouldn’t say I walk around thinking that exactly.\n\nGuilty, maybe in the normal way? Like if I forget to call someone back, or I snap because the kitchen is too crowded, then I’ll replay it longer than I probably need to. But I don’t know if that’s “excessive” or just… being a person in a small apartment with everyone’s habits rubbing up against each other.\n\nHopeless about the future… I don’t really think that far ahead lately. Not in a dramatic way. It’s more like the future is sort of foggy, like when you’re trying to plan dinner but you first have to move three things off the counter, and then you notice the mail pile, and suddenly it’s not about dinner anymore. So… I’m not sure I’d call it hopeless. I haven’t really noticed thoughts like that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Over these past eight months, has this 'muted' feeling actually interfered with your ability to do your job, care for your family, or manage daily responsibilities, or have you still been able to keep up with everything as usual?\n\nPatient: I’m still showing up, mostly. Like, I haven’t disappeared or stopped doing the basic things. I get dressed, I go to work, I answer emails… eventually.\n\nBut it’s not exactly “as usual.” There have been a couple grant deadlines I missed, which is embarrassing because that used to be the kind of thing I could keep in my head, or at least on three different sticky notes. And my supervisor hasn’t said it outright in a harsh way, but she’s… shifted some of my more complicated clients to other clinicians. Quietly. Like she’s trying not to make it a thing.\n\nSo I guess I’m functioning, but not at my normal level. It’s hard to say that without wanting to explain it away.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difference in how you feel on days or times when you're away from the apartment and the caregiving situation—for example, during a weekend trip or a day fully at work—versus when you're at home dealing with it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clean difference like that. I mean, work has its own noise, you know? The printer, people knocking, the little crisis pile that builds up before lunch… so I can’t say I feel suddenly lighter just because I’m not at the apartment.\n\nAnd I haven’t really had, like, a real weekend trip where I could compare it. I keep thinking we should go somewhere, even just a cheap motel with bad coffee, but then there’s the question of who covers what, and whether the cat feeder still works—sorry.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe I’m a little less caught in it when I’m fully occupied somewhere else, but I haven’t tracked it enough to say. At home I’m more aware of everything that needs doing, but that might just be because I’m there looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Focusing just on sleep: compared to eight months ago, are you now taking longer to fall asleep, waking up in the middle of the night, or waking up much earlier than you want to, on most nights?\n\nPatient: Sleep… I’m not sure I have a clean answer for that. I mean, I go to bed, and sometimes I’m thinking about whether I set the coffee up or if the laundry is still in the washer, and then I’ll remember something totally unrelated, like this one client years ago who always wore purple scarves—sorry, that’s not what you asked.\n\nCompared to eight months ago… I haven’t really been tracking it. I don’t think I’m waking up dramatically early every day or anything that obvious. Falling asleep maybe depends on the night? Some nights I’m out, some nights I lie there a while, but I couldn’t honestly say it’s most nights or that it’s a big change.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed a clear sleep pattern change.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your appetite or your weight—either eating more or less than usual, or gaining or losing weight—over these past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? Not in a way I’ve really noticed. My appetite is… kind of ordinary, I guess. Nothing obvious.\n\nDoctor: Regardless of motivation, have you been feeling physically tired or low in energy nearly every day over these past eight months—for example, feeling drained even after resting?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it nearly every day. I mean, I get tired, sure, but everyone says that, don’t they? Especially by the end of the day when there’s still dishes and the mail pile and that one envelope you keep moving from table to table instead of opening.\n\nBut drained even after resting… I’m not sure. Sometimes I rest and then I just feel sort of annoyed that I used up the time resting, which is different from being physically exhausted, I think. Nothing I could point to and say, “Yes, that’s happening almost every day.”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Concentration… sometimes I feel like I’m reading the same paragraph twice, but I don’t know if that’s new or just me being distracted. So, I don’t have a clear answer. Nothing where I thought, “Oh, this is a big change,” exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that you're worthless, or feeling excessively guilty about things, or feeling hopeless about the future during these past eight months?\n\nPatient: Worthless… I don’t know. That’s a heavy word. I wouldn’t say I walk around thinking that exactly.\n\nGuilty, maybe in the normal way? Like if I forget to call someone back, or I snap because the kitchen is too crowded, then I’ll replay it longer than I probably need to. But I don’t know if that’s “excessive” or just… being a person in a small apartment with everyone’s habits rubbing up against each other.\n\nHopeless about the future… I don’t really think that far ahead lately. Not in a dramatic way. It’s more like the future is sort of foggy, like when you’re trying to plan dinner but you first have to move three things off the counter, and then you notice the mail pile, and suddenly it’s not about dinner anymore. So… I’m not sure I’d call it hopeless. I haven’t really noticed thoughts like that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Over these past eight months, has this 'muted' feeling actually interfered with your ability to do your job, care for your family, or manage daily responsibilities, or have you still been able to keep up with everything as usual?\n\nPatient: I’m still showing up, mostly. Like, I haven’t disappeared or stopped doing the basic things. I get dressed, I go to work, I answer emails… eventually.\n\nBut it’s not exactly “as usual.” There have been a couple grant deadlines I missed, which is embarrassing because that used to be the kind of thing I could keep in my head, or at least on three different sticky notes. And my supervisor hasn’t said it outright in a harsh way, but she’s… shifted some of my more complicated clients to other clinicians. Quietly. Like she’s trying not to make it a thing.\n\nSo I guess I’m functioning, but not at my normal level. It’s hard to say that without wanting to explain it away.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difference in how you feel on days or times when you're away from the apartment and the caregiving situation—for example, during a weekend trip or a day fully at work—versus when you're at home dealing with it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clean difference like that. I mean, work has its own noise, you know? The printer, people knocking, the little crisis pile that builds up before lunch… so I can’t say I feel suddenly lighter just because I’m not at the apartment.\n\nAnd I haven’t really had, like, a real weekend trip where I could compare it. I keep thinking we should go somewhere, even just a cheap motel with bad coffee, but then there’s the question of who covers what, and whether the cat feeder still works—sorry.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe I’m a little less caught in it when I’m fully occupied somewhere else, but I haven’t tracked it enough to say. At home I’m more aware of everything that needs doing, but that might just be because I’m there looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Focusing just on sleep: compared to eight months ago, are you now taking longer to fall asleep, waking up in the middle of the night, or waking up much earlier than you want to, on most nights?\n\nPatient: Sleep… I’m not sure I have a clean answer for that. I mean, I go to bed, and sometimes I’m thinking about whether I set the coffee up or if the laundry is still in the washer, and then I’ll remember something totally unrelated, like this one client years ago who always wore purple scarves—sorry, that’s not what you asked.\n\nCompared to eight months ago… I haven’t really been tracking it. I don’t think I’m waking up dramatically early every day or anything that obvious. Falling asleep maybe depends on the night? Some nights I’m out, some nights I lie there a while, but I couldn’t honestly say it’s most nights or that it’s a big change.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed a clear sleep pattern change.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your appetite or your weight—either eating more or less than usual, or gaining or losing weight—over these past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? Anyway, my clothes fit about the same, so I don’t think there’s been a big gain or loss. Nothing obvious.\n\nDoctor: Regardless of motivation, have you been feeling physically tired or low in energy nearly every day over these past eight months—for example, feeling drained even after resting?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it nearly every day. I mean, I get tired, sure, but everyone says that, don’t they? Especially by the end of the day when there’s still dishes and the mail pile and that one envelope you keep moving from table to table instead of opening.\n\nBut drained even after resting… I’m not sure. Sometimes I rest and then I just feel sort of annoyed that I used up the time resting, which is different from being physically exhausted, I think. I haven’t noticed a clear, constant low-energy thing over the whole eight months. Nothing I could point to and say, “Yes, that’s happening almost every day.”\n\nDoctor: {\"intent\": \"Assess for psychomotor changes (a core depressive symptom not yet explored) to determine if enough symptoms are present to meet full criteria for a major depressive episode versus a subthreshold presentation more consistent with adjustment disorder.\", \"question\": \"Have others—or you yourself—noticed you moving or speaking more slow\n\nPatient: Slower… like physically slowed down? I don’t think anyone’s said that to me. I’m not sure how to measure “out of proportion,” honestly. Some days I think, well, of course I’m upset—having someone move in changes the whole rhythm of the house, like where the mugs go and whether the TV is on during dinner, which sounds tiny but it’s not tiny when it’s every day.

I work with people dealing with big life stuff all the time, so I get into this weird comparison thing, like, “This isn’t that bad,” and then I’m annoyed at myself for even thinking that. So… I don’t know. It feels related to the caregiving, definitely. Whether it’s more than a reasonable reaction, I can’t really say.

But I don’t know if that’s “excessive” or just… being a person in a small apartment with everyone’s habits rubbing up against each other.\n\nHopeless about the future… I don’t really think that far ahead lately. Not in a dramatic way. It’s more like the future is sort of foggy, like when you’re trying to plan dinner but you first have to move three things off the counter, and then you notice the mail pile, and suddenly it’s not about dinner anymore. So… I’m not sure I’d call it hopeless. I haven’t really noticed thoughts like that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Over these past eight months, has this 'muted' feeling actually interfered with your ability to do your job, care for your family, or manage daily responsibilities, or have you still been able to keep up with everything as usual?\n\nPatient: I’m still showing up, mostly. Like, I haven’t disappeared or stopped doing the basic things. I get dressed, I go to work, I answer emails… eventually.\n\nBut it’s not exactly “as usual.” There have been a couple grant deadlines I missed, which is embarrassing because that used to be the kind of thing I could keep in my head, or at least on three different sticky notes. And my supervisor hasn’t said it outright in a harsh way, but she’s… shifted some of my more complicated clients to other clinicians. Quietly. Like she’s trying not to make it a thing.\n\nSo I guess I’m functioning, but not at my normal level. It’s hard to say that without wanting to explain it away.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difference in how you feel on days or times when you're away from the apartment and the caregiving situation—for example, during a weekend trip or a day fully at work—versus when you're at home dealing with it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clean difference like that. I mean, work has its own noise, you know? The printer, people knocking, the little crisis pile that builds up before lunch… so I can’t say I feel suddenly lighter just because I’m not at the apartment.\n\nAnd I haven’t really had, like, a real weekend trip where I could compare it. I keep thinking we should go somewhere, even just a cheap motel with bad coffee, but then there’s the question of who covers what, and whether the cat feeder still works—sorry.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe I’m a little less caught in it when I’m fully occupied somewhere else, but I haven’t tracked it enough to say. At home I’m more aware of everything that needs doing, but that might just be because I’m there looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Focusing just on sleep: compared to eight months ago, are you now taking longer to fall asleep, waking up in the middle of the night, or waking up much earlier than you want to, on most nights?\n\nPatient: Sleep… I’m not sure I have a clean answer for that. I mean, I go to bed, and sometimes I’m thinking about whether I set the coffee up or if the laundry is still in the washer, and then I’ll remember something totally unrelated, like this one client years ago who always wore purple scarves—sorry, that’s not what you asked.\n\nCompared to eight months ago… I haven’t really been tracking it. I don’t think I’m waking up dramatically early every day or anything that obvious. Falling asleep maybe depends on the night? Some nights I’m out, some nights I lie there a while, but I couldn’t honestly say it’s most nights or that it’s a big change.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed a clear sleep pattern change.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your appetite or your weight—either eating more or less than usual, or gaining or losing weight—over these past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? Not in a way I’ve really noticed. My appetite is… kind of ordinary, I guess. Some days I forget lunch because the day gets away from me, but that happened before too, especially if I’m doing paperwork and then suddenly it’s 3:40 and the yogurt I brought is still sitting there.\n\nWeight-wise, I don’t weigh myself regularly. We have a scale but it’s under a stack of towels in the bathroom because the closet shelf broke—well, not broke, exactly, it’s just sagging. Anyway, my clothes fit about the same, so I don’t think there’s been a big gain or loss. Nothing obvious.\n\nDoctor: Regardless of motivation, have you been feeling physically tired or low in energy nearly every day over these past eight months—for example, feeling drained even after resting?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it nearly every day. I mean, I get tired, sure, but everyone says that, don’t they? Especially by the end of the day when there’s still dishes and the mail pile and that one envelope you keep moving from table to table instead of opening.\n\nBut drained even after resting… I’m not sure. Sometimes I rest and then I just feel sort of annoyed that I used up the time resting, which is different from being physically exhausted, I think. Nothing I could point to and say, “Yes, that’s happening almost every day.”\n\nDoctor: {\"intent\": \"Assess for psychomotor changes (a core depressive symptom not yet explored) to determine if enough symptoms are present to meet full criteria for a major depressive episode versus a subthreshold presentation more consistent with adjustment disorder.\", \"question\": \"Have others—or you yourself—noticed you moving or speaking more slow\n\nPatient: Slower… like physically slowed down? I don’t think anyone’s said that to me. I mean, people at work will sometimes say I seem distracted, but that’s not the same thing, and honestly everyone seems distracted lately—half our staff meetings are just people looking for the Teams link that was already in the email.\n\nSpeaking more slowly… I haven’t noticed that. I can ramble, obviously, but that’s not new. And moving slowly, no, not in a way I’d point to. I don’t think there’s been some obvious change where I’m dragging around or people are commenting on it.\n\nDoctor: Does the way you've been feeling seem out of proportion to the caregiving stress itself, or does it feel like a reasonable reaction to everything that's changed since your relative moved in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to measure “out of proportion,” honestly. Some days I think, well, of course I’m upset—having someone move in changes the whole rhythm of the house, like where the mugs go and whether the TV is on during dinner, which sounds tiny but it’s not tiny when it’s every day.\n\nBut then I second-guess it, because maybe I should be handling it better. I work with people dealing with big life stuff all the time, so I get into this weird comparison thing, like, “This isn’t that bad,” and then I’m annoyed at myself for even thinking that. So… I don’t know. It feels related to the caregiving, definitely. Whether it’s more than a reasonable reaction, I can’t really say.\n\nDoctor: Over these past eight months, have you had any thoughts of death, of not wanting to be alive, or of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel overwhelmed and think, “I need everything to stop for five minutes,” but not in the sense of wanting to be dead or hurting myself.\n\nIt’s more like wanting quiet, or wanting nobody to ask me where the good towels are, which is… not the same thing. So, no, I haven’t had thoughts of harming myself or not wanting to be alive.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t really know how to answer beyond… I don’t think that’s where my mind has been going. When I get overwhelmed, it’s more like I want the noise and the demands to pause, like when three people are talking at once and the dishwasher is beeping and someone can’t find the insurance card—those ridiculous little pileups.

But I haven’t been sitting there making plans or thinking in that direction, if that’s what you mean. It’s more exhaustion than anything dramatic.

I haven’t really noticed thoughts like that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Over these past eight months, has this 'muted' feeling actually interfered with your ability to do your job, care for your family, or manage daily responsibilities, or have you still been able to keep up with everything as usual?\n\nPatient: I’m still showing up, mostly. Like, I haven’t disappeared or stopped doing the basic things. I keep thinking we should go somewhere, even just a cheap motel with bad coffee, but then there’s the question of who covers what, and whether the cat feeder still works—sorry.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe I’m a little less caught in it when I’m fully occupied somewhere else, but I haven’t tracked it enough to say. I'm Dr. [Name]. To start, can you tell me what's been bringing you in to see me?\n\nPatient: I guess… what finally pushed me to come in is that I don’t feel like myself in this very quiet way. Like the part of me that used to reach for people, or get something back from my work, has sort of gone dim.\n\nI’m still doing what I’m supposed to do, mostly. I go through obligations. But yoga, galleries, journaling—things that used to feel like they filled me up—now I just don’t really… feel much from them. It’s strange, because I used to tell clients all the time about small routines and connection and meaning, and now I’ll buy a new notebook and it just sits there. Actually, I have this whole drawer of notebooks, which is ridiculous, but anyway… that’s the main thing. I feel like I’m moving through things rather than living in them.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—weeks, months, or longer?\n\nPatient: I’d say… months. About eight months, I think. It started around when we took my older relative into our apartment, and since then the whole way I’m thinking and working and being with people just feels different. I keep trying to pinpoint a day, but it wasn’t really like that—it was more like the apartment changed, the routines changed, and then I looked up and realized I’d changed too.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the loss of interest and pleasure, have you also been feeling sad, empty, or down for most of the day, nearly every day, during these past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. I’ll have moments where I’m fine, like if I’m making tea or noticing some weird little thing outside the window, and then I think, “See, I’m okay.” But then the rest of the day feels kind of flat.\n\nSorry, I’m not answering cleanly. I guess I haven’t been crying all the time or anything. It’s more just… not much seems to land.\n\nDoctor: Besides feeling muted or flat, have you noticed changes in your energy, concentration, sleep, or appetite over these past eight months?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure I can separate those out very well. Like, energy versus motivation versus just being tired of the same rooms, you know?\n\nSleep—well, I notice it more when my neighbor’s hallway light flickers under the door, which is such a small thing, but once I see it I can’t unsee it. And then I start thinking about whether we should change the curtains, or whether the plant near the window is dying because I moved it. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t been tracking appetite or anything. Nothing where I thought, “Oh, this is a big change,” exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that you're worthless, or feeling excessively guilty about things, or feeling hopeless about the future during these past eight months?\n\nPatient: Worthless… I don’t know. That’s a heavy word. I wouldn’t say I walk around thinking that exactly.\n\nGuilty, maybe in the normal way? Like if I forget to call someone back, or I snap because the kitchen is too crowded, then I’ll replay it longer than I probably need to. Not in a dramatic way. It’s more like the future is sort of foggy, like when you’re trying to plan dinner but you first have to move three things off the counter, and then you notice the mail pile, and suddenly it’s not about dinner anymore. So… I’m not sure I’d call it hopeless. I haven’t really noticed thoughts like that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Over these past eight months, has this 'muted' feeling actually interfered with your ability to do your job, care for your family, or manage daily responsibilities, or have you still been able to keep up with everything as usual?\n\nPatient: I’m still showing up, mostly. Like, I haven’t disappeared or stopped doing the basic things. I get dressed, I go to work, I answer emails… eventually.\n\nBut it’s not exactly “as usual.” There have been a couple grant deadlines I missed, which is embarrassing because that used to be the kind of thing I could keep in my head, or at least on three different sticky notes. Like she’s trying not to make it a thing.\n\nSo I guess I’m functioning, but not at my normal level. It’s hard to say that without wanting to explain it away.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difference in how you feel on days or times when you're away from the apartment and the caregiving situation—for example, during a weekend trip or a day fully at work—versus when you're at home dealing with it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clean difference like that. I mean, work has its own noise, you know? The printer, people knocking, the little crisis pile that builds up before lunch… so I can’t say I feel suddenly lighter just because I’m not at the apartment.\n\nAnd I haven’t really had, like, a real weekend trip where I could compare it. I keep thinking we should go somewhere, even just a cheap motel with bad coffee, but then there’s the question of who covers what, and whether the cat feeder still works—sorry.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe I’m a little less caught in it when I’m fully occupied somewhere else, but I haven’t tracked it enough to say. At home I’m more aware of everything that needs doing, but that might just be because I’m there looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Focusing just on sleep: compared to eight months ago, are you now taking longer to fall asleep, waking up in the middle of the night, or waking up much earlier than you want to, on most nights?\n\nPatient: Sleep… I’m not sure I have a clean answer for that. I mean, I go to bed, and sometimes I’m thinking about whether I set the coffee up or if the laundry is still in the washer, and then I’ll remember something totally unrelated, like this one client years ago who always wore purple scarves—sorry, that’s not what you asked.\n\nCompared to eight months ago… I haven’t really been tracking it. I don’t think I’m waking up dramatically early every day or anything that obvious. Falling asleep maybe depends on the night? Some nights I’m out, some nights I lie there a while, but I couldn’t honestly say it’s most nights or that it’s a big change.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed a clear sleep pattern change.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your appetite or your weight—either eating more or less than usual, or gaining or losing weight—over these past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? Not in a way I’ve really noticed. My appetite is… kind of ordinary, I guess. Some days I forget lunch because the day gets away from me, but that happened before too, especially if I’m doing paperwork and then suddenly it’s 3:40 and the yogurt I brought is still sitting there.\n\nWeight-wise, I don’t weigh myself regularly. We have a scale but it’s under a stack of towels in the bathroom because the closet shelf broke—well, not broke, exactly, it’s just sagging. Anyway, my clothes fit about the same, so I don’t think there’s been a big gain or loss. Nothing obvious.\n\nDoctor: Regardless of motivation, have you been feeling physically tired or low in energy nearly every day over these past eight months—for example, feeling drained even after resting?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it nearly every day. I haven’t noticed a clear, constant low-energy thing over the whole eight months. Nothing I could point to and say, “Yes, that’s happening almost every day.”\n\nDoctor: {\"intent\": \"Assess for psychomotor changes (a core depressive symptom not yet explored) to determine if enough symptoms are present to meet full criteria for a major depressive episode versus a subthreshold presentation more consistent with adjustment disorder.\", \"question\": \"Have others—or you yourself—noticed you moving or speaking more slow\n\nPatient: Slower… like physically slowed down? At home I’m more aware of everything that needs doing, but that might just be because I’m there looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Focusing just on sleep: compared to eight months ago, are you now taking longer to fall asleep, waking up in the middle of the night, or waking up much earlier than you want to, on most nights?\n\nPatient: Sleep… I’m not sure I have a clean answer for that. I mean, I go to bed, and sometimes I’m thinking about whether I set the coffee up or if the laundry is still in the washer, and then I’ll remember something totally unrelated, like this one client years ago who always wore purple scarves—sorry, that’s not what you asked.\n\nCompared to eight months ago… I haven’t really been tracking it. I don’t think I’m waking up dramatically early every day or anything that obvious. Falling asleep maybe depends on the night? Some nights I’m out, some nights I lie there a while, but I couldn’t honestly say it’s most nights or that it’s a big change.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed a clear sleep pattern change.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your appetite or your weight—either eating more or less than usual, or gaining or losing weight—over these past eight months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? Not in a way I’ve really noticed. My appetite is… kind of ordinary, I guess. Some days I forget lunch because the day gets away from me, but that happened before too, especially if I’m doing paperwork and then suddenly it’s 3:40 and the yogurt I brought is still sitting there.\n\nWeight-wise, I don’t weigh myself regularly. We have a scale but it’s under a stack of towels in the bathroom because the closet shelf broke—well, not broke, exactly, it’s just sagging. Anyway, my clothes fit about the same, so I don’t think there’s been a big gain or loss. Nothing obvious.\n\nDoctor: Regardless of motivation, have you been feeling physically tired or low in energy nearly every day over these past eight months—for example, feeling drained even after resting?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it nearly every day. I mean, I get tired, sure, but everyone says that, don’t they? Especially by the end of the day when there’s still dishes and the mail pile and that one envelope you keep moving from table to table instead of opening.\n\nBut drained even after resting… I’m not sure. Sometimes I rest and then I just feel sort of annoyed that I used up the time resting, which is different from being physically exhausted, I think. I haven’t noticed a clear, constant low-energy thing over the whole eight months. Nothing I could point to and say, “Yes, that’s happening almost every day.”\n\nDoctor: {\"intent\": \"Assess for psychomotor changes (a core depressive symptom not yet explored) to determine if enough symptoms are present to meet full criteria for a major depressive episode versus a subthreshold presentation more consistent with adjustment disorder.\", \"question\": \"Have others—or you yourself—noticed you moving or speaking more slow\n\nPatient: Slower… like physically slowed down? I don’t think anyone’s said that to me. I mean, people at work will sometimes say I seem distracted, but that’s not the same thing, and honestly everyone seems distracted lately—half our staff meetings are just people looking for the Teams link that was already in the email.\n\nSpeaking more slowly… I haven’t noticed that. I can ramble, obviously, but that’s not new. And moving slowly, no, not in a way I’d point to. I don’t think there’s been some obvious change where I’m dragging around or people are commenting on it.\n\nDoctor: Does the way you've been feeling seem out of proportion to the caregiving stress itself, or does it feel like a reasonable reaction to everything that's changed since your relative moved in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to measure “out of proportion,” honestly. Some days I think, well, of course I’m upset—having someone move in changes the whole rhythm of the house, like where the mugs go and whether the TV is on during dinner, which sounds tiny but it’s not tiny when it’s every day.\n\nBut then I second-guess it, because maybe I should be handling it better. I work with people dealing with big life stuff all the time, so I get into this weird comparison thing, like, “This isn’t that bad,” and then I’m annoyed at myself for even thinking that. So… I don’t know. It feels related to the caregiving, definitely. Whether it’s more than a reasonable reaction, I can’t really say.\n\nDoctor: Over these past eight months, have you had any thoughts of death, of not wanting to be alive, or of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel overwhelmed and think, “I need everything to stop for five minutes,” but not in the sense of wanting to be dead or hurting myself.\n\nIt’s more like wanting quiet, or wanting nobody to ask me where the good towels are, which is… not the same thing. So, no, I haven’t had thoughts of harming myself or not wanting to be alive.\n\nDoctor: \n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer beyond… I don’t think that’s where my mind has been going. When I get overwhelmed, it’s more like I want the noise and the demands to pause, like when three people are talking at once and the dishwasher is beeping and someone can’t find the insurance card—those ridiculous little pileups.\n\nBut I haven’t been sitting there making plans or thinking in that direction, if that’s what you mean. It’s more exhaustion than anything dramatic.\n\nDoctor: \n\nPatient: I’m not sure what you’re asking me there—if you mean whether there’s more to it than being tired and irritated, I don’t know. It’s hard to separate out, because everything kind of blends together after a while.\n\nLike, I’ll start thinking I’m upset about one thing, and then suddenly I’m remembering that the hallway closet still has boxes in it from when they moved in, and then I’m mad about the boxes instead of the original thing. So… I may need you to ask it a little more directly."
  }

Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking. Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli. Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health. Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI’m not sure I’ve noticed a clean difference like that. I mean, work has its own noise, you know? The printer, people knocking, the little crisis pile that builds up before lunch… so I can’t say I feel suddenly lighter just because I’m not at the apartment.\n\nAnd I haven’t really had, like, a real weekend trip where I could compare it. Maybe I’m a little less caught in it when I’m fully occupied somewhere else, but I haven’t tracked it enough to say. At home I’m more aware of everything that needs doing, but that might just be because I’m there looking at it.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.
